### Page 1

1

SPECIAL EDITION | HOW BAS IS THE RISE 
IN BOND YIELDS?

TRANSCRIPTION

0:00:10 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Everyone, welcome. As we head into autumn 2026, interest rates are reaching levels never seen for many, many years. 
Why is this happening? Is it sustainable? And what are the implications for the economies of different countries? All these 
questions will be discussed over the next few minutes with the economists of BNP Paribas. Let us begin with Isabelle 
Mateos y Lago, chief economist of the group. Hello, Isabelle.  
0:00:38 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Hello Emmanuel, hello everyone. 
0:00:39 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you for being with us today. Just to begin, a simple explanation. What rates are we talking about and which countries 
are affected? 
0:00:48 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Yes, it is very important to clarify this. So, we are talking about long-term interest rates, rates at 10 years or longer 
maturities, 30 years, etc. The rates at which sovereign states borrow on the market. And this is primarily something that is 
happening in advanced countries, so United States, United King dom, Japan, and then the Eurozone countries. And indeed, 
as you said, we are seeing since the beginning of the summer, interest rates reach levels we had not seen for about 20 
years. But it is important to keep in mind that up until that point, up until the mid -2000s, the level of interest rates that 
we're seeing now were really quite common, quite normal, so one shouldn't overdramatize.  
0:01:35 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Why do we find ourselves in this situation and how did we get to this point?  
0:01:41 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Yes, so the direct cause is that investors are demanding higher interest rates in order to  lend their capital to governments. 
But why is this? Well, there's essentially three reasons for this, and not all of them are bad, and they're not mutually 
exclusive either. The top reason to my mind is that there's just greater competition for capital be tween sovereign states 
and the corporate sector, who are all facing simultaneously additional investment needs, in defense, in artificial 
intelligence, in energy transition, in resilience. And so,  you just have greater competition for capital and that pushes up the 
price of capital, which is the interest rate. 
Reason number two is because markets have revised upwards their expectations of how high central banks are going to be 
taking their interest rates. 
And this mechanically translates into higher rates at the long end. And then there's a third reason, which doesn't apply 
necessarily in every case, but which is essentially higher sovereign risk, a kind of a risk premium, in cases where there are  
concerns arising about the sustainability of these public debts.

### Page 2

2

0:02:59 - Emmanuel Laborde 
What are the implications for global economies? 
0:03:03 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
So that's exactly what we'll be discussing in the next segments. But basically, the two key issues are, one, an adverse 
impact on public finances. It's higher interest rates making it harder to balance the books in terms of public accounts. And 
then secondly, these higher rates tend to slow down economic growth. Of course, by how much and how serious it will be, 
depends on how high interest rates go and how long they stay there. But that's for the next segments.  
0:03:38 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Yes. Thank you very much, Isabelle. Having said the scene now, let's move on to the first segment with the following 
question. What makes debt sustainable? And let's see what Hélène Baudchon has to say.  
Hello Hélène and welcome into this special edition. 
0:03:58 - Hélène Baudchon 
Hello Emmanuel, hello everybody. 
0:04:01 - Emmanuel Laborde 
When economists talk about public debt, they often refer to the sustainability of debt. As an economist, can you explain to 
us what that means and what is the debt trajectory deemed unsustainable?  
0:04:15 - Hélène Baudchon 
To begin with, I will say that a temporary increase in debt can be seen as a normal evolution, not particularly concerning, 
for instance during an economic crisis or more recently during a health crisis. In such cases, it acts as a buffer. A problem  
arises when the debt level never comes back down. An unsustainable trajectory can be therefore defined, characterized by 
a continuous regular rise in the debt level, both in absolute terms and as a proportion of GDP, driven by high and recurring 
fiscal deficits. And sometimes interest payments can exacerbate the problem.  
0:05:00 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In very simple terms, you are talking about the snowball effect.  
0:05:03 - Hélène Baudchon 
Exactly explaining why consolidation measures are needed in order to prevent such an adverse situation from occurring 
and in order to prevent, in fact, a sudden loss of confidence from creditors.  
0:05:16 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Regarding governments, what should serve as a warning sign for them? And at what point could the situation become, let's 
say, problematic? 
0:05:27 - Hélène Baudchon 
Well, simply speaking, when interest rates rise, not so long ago, up until 2022, Eurozone countries were borrowing at very 
low rates and in some instance, even negative rates. And although debt levels were already at high level at that time, the 
low rates helped manage debt servicing. Not much fiscal effort was needed to keep the public debt ratios in check.  
0:05:55 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Of course, we all remember this because it was a very unusual situation, but now things are very different.

### Page 3

3

0:06:01 - Hélène Baudchon 
Yes, indeed, the situation today is clearly more challenging simply because bond yields are significantly higher and they 
are rising at the time of our discussion. This is for the part the result of the ECB's monetary tightening in 2022 -2023 aimed 
at addressing the inflation shock then. Then long-term interest rates have risen as a consequence and have aligned more 
closely with GDP growth rate. And in technical terms, this is what we refer to as a narrowing of the "r" - "g" spread. 
0:06:41 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Why is this spread so important? Why do you, the economists, monitor it this way?  
0:06:48 - Hélène Baudchon 
Essentially, you have the interest rate "r", which determines the debt burden, while the growth rate "g" determines the 
trajectory of fiscal revenues. And if "r" is lower than "g", and this has been the case for many years, as you can see on the  
chart on the screen, it means that interest payments are increasing at a slower pace than the revenue component of the 
equation. 
0:07:14 - Emmanuel Laborde 
And this provides some leeway in other budgetary areas, such as education, of course, defense, maybe pensions.  
0:07:22 - Hélène Baudchon 
Yes, indeed. And this is what we refer to as the primary budget. However, if and when interest rates do go up while the 
economy isn't growing that much faster, then fiscal leeway diminishes.  
0:07:35 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Crystal clear. Now, just could you provide a few last words to wrap up the sustainability segment?  
0:07:43 - Hélène Baudchon 
Well, to conclude, we are entering a period which demands greater fiscal discipline or, as some might say, less indiscipline 
in order to manage debt trajectories effectively, in particular in the countries which have high debt levels. And for instanc e, 
the Italian case is an interesting one because it is well known for its high debt level ratio. But in fact, it also has an 
advantageous position because it has no primary deficit. In fact, it even benefits from a large primary surplus.  
0:08:22 - Emmanuel Laborde 
As for the countries... For the other countries... I stop you because this will be a discussion with Guillaume Derrien during  
the next segment. Thank you very much, Hélène. 
0:08:30 - Hélène Baudchon 
Thank you, Emmanuel. 
0:08:36 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Hello, Guillaume. Welcome. 
0:08:37 - Guillaume Derrien 
Thank you. 
0:08:38 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Hello, Emmanuel, and hello to everyone. The flexibility of fiscal policy depends heavily on interest rates, which have 
increased considerably over the past few months. The question is, where do we stand today?

4

0:08:52 - Guillaume Derrien 
So, long-term rates have increased in all advanced economies, reaching levels not seen since 2008 in Europe and in the 
United States, and even since 1996 in Japan. Fortunately, the effective rate that states pay on their debt is lower than thos e 
headline figures suggest, simply because the average maturity of debt is relatively long. Precisely how long are we talking 
about? So, it's about 10 years in Japan, 13 years in the United Kingdom, and 8.5 years in France. That means that most of 
the debt was raised before rates started climbing. 
For example, in France, the effective rate just recently rose above the 2% mark, which is significantly lower than the 10 -
year bond yield, which exceeded 4% for new issuances.  
That said, in the United States, which has an average maturity of roughly six years, is experiencing a more rapid increase in  
its effective rate. 
0:09:54 - Emmanuel Laborde 
And this rate increase is happening in the context of high debt -to-GDP ratios and with modest growth, correct? Yes, that's 
correct. 
0:10:05 - Guillaume Derrien 
Debt levels have reached unprecedented heights since the end of World War II. And at the same time, growth is lower, 
hovering around 2% on average in the United States, 1% in Europe and 0.5% in Japan. What does that mean for debt 
servicing costs? So, debt servicing cost is already relatively high, but it is expected to grow further as debt issue before the 
2022 rate hikes mature and are replaced with new instruments bearing higher interest rates. By 2030, interest costs could 
rise to levels last seen in 2000, specifically in France, around 4% of GDP. 
Are all the countries equally affected by these trends? Actually, no. Some countries are managing to reduce their debt 
level. 
Spain, Italy, even Japan have sufficiently improved their primary balance to achieve this, at least for the time being. And o f 
course, we must speak about Germany. Yes, Germany is implementing investment programs that result in a higher public 
deficit and debt-to-GDP ratio. But this ratio started from a relatively low base at around above 60% of GDP. That said, in 
this current context, German bonds have risen. And because German bonds are used as a benchmark for Europe, it  raises 
rates across all Eurozone countries. Is France the only country seeing its public debt go up?  
No, it's not, Emmanuel. This is happening in the United Kingdom and in the United States. These economies, like France, 
are running public deficits that are ever close or exceed 5% of GDP, which is too large for debt ratio to stabilize on its ow n. 
France and the United Kingdom are trying to gradually reduce their public deficit so as not to compromise growth. The 
United States is not going on that path yet, but market pressure could eventually force them to do so.  
0:12:08 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much for your input, Guillaume. Thank you, Emmanuel. Let's go to the next segment. It's about the impact 
of rising bond yields on financing and investments in the Eurozone.  
0:12:26 - Thomas Humblot 
Hello, Thomas. Welcome. Hello. Thank you for having me today.  
0:12:30 - Emmanuel Laborde 
We've just seen what's behind the rise in Eurozone bond yields. This rise is partly attributed to the sovereign spread. What 
impact will this have on the economy? 
0:12:42 - Thomas Humblot 
Actually, this widening of some government bond spreads is obviously not good news, but it tends paradoxically to have 
less impact than an increase in long-term interest rates that does not include the sovereign risk premium. for example, in 
France, for a very long time, it has been considered that the 10 -year OAT was the relevant benchmark for fixed loans. And 
at that time, the spread on French government bonds was relatively stable. But since 2024, that spread increased

### Page 5

5

significantly and that renders the sovereign yield a less relevant benchmark.  
And now the most relevant benchmark is the swap rate because it determines the cost of bank funding that backs new 
loans. 
0:13:56 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Since the end of 2024, we have seen a rise in long-term 10-year swap rates. What impact does this have on bank lending 
rates? 
0:14:08 - Thomas Humblot 
Fixed loans have obviously become more expensive, but to a lesser extent than if they were priced solely based on long -
term sovereign rates. And what about existing fixed-rate loans? 
There is no impact. An increase in rates for the entire maturity of a loan does not have any effect if it is a fixed -rate loan. 
Borrowers with outstanding loans are therefore protected. Obviously, new fixed loans are granted at higher rates. And this 
sharply contrasts with a floating-rates loans. 
Because with floating-rates loans, borrowers experience regular rate adjustments throughout the term of the loan. And in 
terms of bank financing? Strictly in terms of bank financing, an increase in short -term rates has an immediate and 
widespread restrictive impact on borrowers, while for long-term interest rates, the impact is more gradual, and it comes 
gradually as the outstanding amount of fixed-rate loans are progressively renewed. And the thing is that since the 
beginning of the war in Iran, we have seen a combination of the two.  
And in practical terms, the rate of new bank loans to non-financial corporations has increased about 25 basis points in the 
eurozone since the beginning of the year. 
0:16:12 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Just a few words about companies that borrow directly from the market.  
0:16:16 - Thomas Humblot 
Well, in that case, the impact is even more straightforward. The rate at which a company borrows from the market is the 
risk-free rate plus a premium. 
0:16:28 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Have risk premiums, known as corporate credit spreads, also increased?  
0:16:34 - Thomas Humblot 
Yes, they have increased temporarily at the beginning of the year but have returned to normal since. For the time being, the 
widening remains moderate for the highest-rated investment-grade issuers. 
However, some significant bond issuance in the euro debt market due to some major US technology companies has 
increased spreads for longer maturities. 
0:17:11 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In particular terms, how does this affect the investment decisions of the companies?  
0:17:19 - Thomas Humblot 
Well, investments that were previously worthwhile due to returns sufficient to cover the cost of capital may no longer do so 
and in turn that might lead to a decrease in demand for financing.  
0:17:40 - Emmanuel Laborde

Hello Cynthia, and welcome. 
0:17:54 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Hi, Emmanuel. Hello, everyone. 
0:17:57 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Long-term bond yields rose significantly in advanced economies over the summer. What can we now say about emerging 
markets? 
0:18:06 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Emerging bond markets held up well, which is quite unusual. The rise in long -term sovereign yields in emerging countries 
has been less pronounced than in advanced economies, including the United States and Germany. Now, there are two 
main exceptions, Turkey and Poland. Meanwhile, we did not witness any significant correction in emerging financial 
markets over the summer, and the main currencies appreciated slightly against the dollar or the euro.  
0:18:37 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Does this mean that public finances are more robust in emerging countries?  
0:18:43 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, it depends. You see, in cases like the Czech Republic and South Korea, public debt is moderate and macroeconomic 
fundamentals are solid. However, these countries are now less frequently considered as emerging and tend to be viewed 
as newly advanced economies. By contrast, Brazil and South Africa have weaker public finances and investors have 
already incorporated a higher sovereign risk into their assessments. And this is reflected in the bond yields. And this 
assessment of sovereign risk did not really change over the summer. 
0:19:26 - Emmanuel Laborde 
When comparing the main fiscal metrics, what are the trends? What do you see as an economist?  
0:19:33 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
On average, the fiscal metrics in emerging markets are not substantially worse than those in advanced economies. The 
average budget deficit levels are similar, ranging from 4% to 5% of GDP in 2025 -2026, according to the IMF. And as in 
advanced economies, the public debt is higher than its pre-Covid level. However, the average public debt to GDP ratio in 
emerging countries excluding China is close to 60%, whereas in advanced economies, it is above 100%.  
0:20:12 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Do you arrive at the same conclusion when comparing debt sustainability criteria?  
0:20:17 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, there are significant regional differences. In emerging Asia, the factors that determine debt sustainability are more 
often favorable than those in advanced economies. In Central Europe, the gap between the interest rate paid on debt and 
the GDP growth rate is also favorable. However, the primary deficits in some countries are high.  
0:20:45 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In a few words, a brief overview of the situation in Latin America.  
0:20:49 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, Brazil and Mexico are in a less favorable position. These countries would indeed need to generate a significant 
primary budget surplus each year in order to stabilize their debt in the medium term.

### Page 7

0:21:07 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Economists use the term original sin, which refers to the situation when governments resort to borrowing in foreign 
currency to address the shortfall in domestic financing. Does this phenomenon continue to distinguish emerging 
countries? 
0:21:24 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Yes, this is still the case. Emerging countries still have a significant share of their debt in foreign currency. This repres ents 
about 35 to 40% of their total debt on average. And in fact, now, this figure has decreased over the past decade, and this 
contributes to their resilience. However, governments in emerging markets have a less favorable position as regards their 
debt profile and their financing costs are also on average higher.  
0:22:02 - Emmanuel Laborde 
And finally, just a few words about a larger subject, China.  
0:22:07 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
China's public debt is significant and growing rapidly. That being said, it is denominated in domestic currency and issued in  
domestic markets. This debt is, however, sustainable in the medium term due to a very low effective interest rate and a 
high growth rate. 
0:22:29 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much, Cynthia, for being with us today.  
0:22:32 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Thank you. 
0:22:33 - Emmanuel Laborde 
It is now time to conclude, and we welcome back Group Chief Economist Isabelle Mateos y Lago.  
It is time for our conclusion with Isabelle. Once again, just a final question, please. Could or should, I do not know which 
word is the good one, central banks intervene to curb this rise in interest rates?  
0:22:58 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Absolutely not. The main contribution that central banks can bring to dealing with this situation is to restore price stabili ty, 
as is their mandate, as promptly as possible. And in the current circumstances, this means keeping relatively high policy 
rates, in some cases, rising them further. And the responsibility for keeping public finances on a sustainable footing rests 
squarely with governments and any attempt by these governments to instead take the easy way out by pressuring central 
banks to lower interest rates prematurely would be enormously counterproductive.  
0:23:41 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much, Isabelle. Thank you all for joining us today. Just a few words to remind you that additional economic

13028742     1

Economy 
Insurance costs hit the brakes 
16 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Property insurance (e.g., auto, homeowners, renters) payment growth has slowed considerably, according to Bank of America 
payments data. Median payments rose just 1% year-over-year (YoY) in 2026, easing further from the 6% growth seen in 2025, 
after a roughly 12% increase in 2024, suggesting that many consumers are getting some relief from rising insurance payments. 
• However, the moderation is uneven across consumers. Bank of America payments data suggests that Gen Z continues to 
experience insurance payment growth of nearly 6%, while increases for other generations are closer to 1% to 2%. Payments 
also rose nearly 1.5% for higher-income households but edged lower for those with lower incomes. 
• Lower accident rates, easing vehicle prices, and softer major-purchase growth may be reducing cost pressures, but geography 
remains a major dividing line. Florida metros recorded declines of roughly 7% to 8%, while California and several Northeastern 
metros continued to see relatively strong increases.

Insurance payments stabilize after years of painful growth 
The burden of higher property (e.g., auto, renters, homeowners) insurance payments may be easing for some. Notably, while the 
average payment increased 3% year-over-year (YoY), the median payment was only up 1%, according to Bank of America 
payments data. This suggests that most consumers have only seen a small increase in their premiums, though there may be a 
few outliers (Exhibit 1).  
The latest data stands in contrast to just two years ago in 2024, when most consumers saw their insurance payments surge 
about 12% YoY, after a nearly 8% YoY increase in 2023.   
Exhibit 1: Insurance payments have increased only modestly through August of this year, with growth peaking in 2024 
Median and average insurance payments (September to August yearly, YoY%)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Insurance payments by age show a similar pattern, with much stronger growth in 2024 across most generations followed by a 
cooling since then (Exhibit 2). Gen Z is the outlier here, with their payments still growing at a healthy rate, increasing almost 6% 
YoY for both 2026 and 2025, after a 7% increase in 2024. Meanwhile, Millennials and Baby Boomers experienced a 2% YoY 
increase so far this year, with Gen X only seeing around a 1% rise.

Bank of America payments data by income provides even more insight. Higher-income households paid nearly 1.5% more in 
2026 than they did a year ago, while middle-income households saw only modest increases or none at all (Exhibit 3). Conversely, 
lower-income households actually paid slightly less so far this year than they did last year.  
Exhibit 3: Higher-income households are still paying more for insurance compared to last year, while lower earners are paying less 
Median insurance payments by income cuts (September to August yearly, YoY%)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Fewer accidents and lower vehicles prices may be driving insurance payments down 
But why might insurance payments be declining at all? One thing to keep in mind is that insurance companies often raise rates 
to cover events that occurred in previous years to recover costs, while adjusting for changing risks.  
For example, the crash rate – the number of vehicle crashes per one million miles driven – suggests that there was a sharp rise 
in accidents in 2021, according to the Department of Transportation (Exhibit 4). Additionally, new and used vehicle prices 
increased significantly after the pandemic, according to Consumer Price Index (CPI) data from the Bureau of Labor Statistics 
(BLS) (Exhibit 5). So, insurers were not only facing more vehicle replacements due to a higher crash rate, but a higher cost to 
replace vehicles as well.

However, the crash rate mostly declined from 2021 to 2024, and the Department of Transportation reported that the number of 
accidents likely decreased again in 2025. Also, while the cost to maintain and repair vehicles has consistently grown since 2021, 
the price to replace vehicles peaked in 2022 and has eased since then – the latter likely a more important factor in insurance 
costs than the former. Additionally, while the CPI index for motor vehicle insurance has fallen 5% YoY, it’s possible that this may 
partially be due to rebates, according to BofA Global Research. So, the price figured into CPI may not be fully realized by the 
consumer.

Another factor in the divergence between a declining CPI and rising insurance payments in our data may be the growth in the 
amount of property people are insuring now. Notably though, this growth has eased in recent years. YoY growth in both new 
home and total vehicle sales peaked in mid-2023 and has grown modestly, or even declined, since (Exhibit 6). And given that 
these major purchases are still K-shaped (read more in Autos: In gear, but the “K” is still in the headlights and On the move: 
Renovation over relocation?), it could help to explain the divergence in insurance payment growth across income cohorts.

Florida’s metros have seen the largest decline when compared to other major cities 
Examining different cities across the US also sheds light on insurance trends. Consumers in major California metros saw the 
largest increases in 2026, following near double digit growth in 2025, likely driven by widespread property damage from 
wildfires, according to BofA Global Research (Exhibit 7). Major coastal metros in the Northeast like Boston and New York also 
experienced sizable increases as well.  
Conversely, many cities throughout the Sun Belt like Houston, Nashville and Dallas saw flat or even declining insurance 
payments. BofA Global Research points to several reasons for these declines: part of the equation in this region is that the 
massive runup in the cost of property insurance and domestic migration to the region through 2024 has since eased or reversed 
course.   
Exhibit 7: California major metros have seen steady insurance payment growth, while Miami metros have seen declines 
Median insurance payments by core based statistical areas (September to August yearly, YoY%)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
In Florida, legislation that went into effect in 2023, eventually made its way into insurance prices in 2025, leading to widespread 
declines in premiums. The state also has a “lookback period” of three years, where insurance profits can be deemed too high and 
a rebate is issued to consumers. It’s possible this rebate is factoring into declines this year, as some carriers only allow rebates 
upon renewals.  
The Sunshine State was a notable standout for declines, with Orlando, Tampa and Jacksonville each down around 8% YoY in 
2026, followed closely by Miami with a 7% decrease. Additionally, Bank of America payments data suggests that Jacksonville, 
Miami and Orlando all had median insurance payments above the national level in 2023, though their payments were fell below 
that in 2026 (Exhibit 8).  
Major metros in Texas also saw easing insurance payments relative to 2023, while Seattle, San Jose, and Riverside, CA are 
among the major metros that went from below the national median to above in the three-year period ending in 2026.

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Insurance payments include renters, auto or home, but do not include insurance escrowed into mortgage payments or deducted from a 
paycheck.  
We consider a measure of services necessity spending that includes but is not limited to groceries, utilities, and gasoline. Discretionary services 
includes but is not limited to charitable donations, leisure travel, entertainment, and professional/consumer services. Discretionary retail 
includes but is not limited to general merchandise, miscellaneous, clothing, electronics and furniture. It excludes categories like groceries and 
gasoline. Holiday spending is defined as items in which spending in the November-December period is usually at least 20% of total annual 
spending on the category.  Value and premium grocers are determined judgmentally on a proprietary analysis of merchants by equity analysts. 
The “more discretionary” retail category includes furniture, electronics, building materials, online marketplaces, clothing retailers and 
miscellaneous stores; while “partly discretionary” retail contains auto parts and services, health and personal care retailers, general merchandise 
and “mostly necessity”  retail contains gas and groceries. 
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

Palantir 新簽訂單放緩了？蘋果服務
被 AI 影響了？財報焦點 : Palantir +
Apple 蘋果 | 26 年 10 ⽉
KP@FOMOSOC AND BENITA HSU
NOV 3 ∙ PAID
READ IN APP
這次我們想做個新嘗試。
之前幾期的「財報解剖」⼀向以極致的深度著稱，但⽼實說，並⾮每次財報公
佈，都有必要把所有維度拉出來重新解構⼀遍。
有些時候，最關鍵的變化其實只集中在兩三個核⼼變數上。
因此，我們推出了這個更聚焦、節奏更快的變體版。

雖然篇幅縮減，但我們對商業本質的思考沒有打折。這裡保留了最精華的洞
察，保證依然⽐市⾯上絕⼤多數的財報分析更有⾒解。
這份分析由我們的分析師  Benita 主導撰寫，我負責校正觀點並做最後把關，希
望能⽤更⾼效的⽅式，幫⼤家抓出最值得關注的產業訊號。
Palantir ⼜⼀次完美財報，企業 AI
開始真正變現，但新簽訂單卻放緩了？

### Page 2

Palantir 第三季⼜交出了⼀份近乎沒有瑕疵的財報。
2026 年第三季營收來到  23.13 億美元，年增  96% 、季增  20% ，創下公司史上
最⾼的年增率，同時也是連續第  13 個季度營收成⻑加速。

### Page 3

其中美國商業營收⼤增  148% ，調整後營業利益率從去年同期的  51% 躍升⾄
62% ，推升  Rule of 40 達到  158% 。
同時，公司還把第四季營收指引調⾼到約  26.51 億美元，⾼於市場原本預期的
約  24.7 億美元（⾼出約  7% ）。
只要第四季順利達成指引， Palantir 的年化營收規模就將正式跨過  100 億美元
⼤關。
不過， Palantir 的「好」，已經不是新聞了。
我們會將這次的分析分為 4 個重點。
重點⼀：企業 AI 開始真正變現 —— 從百萬美元試點，⾛向千萬美
元的營運系統
過去這兩年，許多企業在導⼊ AI 時，最常⾒的做法就是去買⼤模型的 API 、做
個內部聊天機器⼈，或是花錢做⼀堆概念驗證（ PoC ）。
但試了⼀圈之後，許多企業開始感到挫折。
⼀⽅⾯，對外串接純⼤模型供應商，很難完全保護公司內部的機密資料與營運
知識；另⼀⽅⾯，讓 AI 寫寫信、做個摘要並不難，真正的挑戰在於如何讓 AI
融⼊公司的⽇常⼯作流程，並創造出⾜以回本的經濟效益。

為了解決這個痛點， Palantir 過去透過實戰⼯作坊（ AIP Bootcamp ，近期已進⼀
步演進為打造 AI 代理的 AgentCamp ），讓企業帶著⾃⼰的真實資料，在短時間
內做出⼀套能實際運作的系統。
到了今年，這套模式更延伸到「主權 AI （ Sovereign AI ）」。
這裡談的 AI 主權，重點在於讓企業⾃⼰掌握資料、業務邏輯與模型選擇權。 企
業可以⾃由更換底層模型，並把真正值錢的營運知識留在⾃⼰的系統裡。
在這個架構下，企業可以利⽤⾃⼰累積的營運資料，在 Palantir 的 Ontology （本
體論）架構下評估並微調開放權重模型，完全不必每件事都去呼叫最昂貴的頂
級⼤模型。
在這次法說會上，有兩個案例特別有說服⼒：
第⼀個是國防科技公司 L3Harris 。

### Page 4

他們利⽤⾃家資料微調開放模型後，在不到 48 ⼩時內，就讓這套主權模型的表
現超越了原本使⽤的前沿⼤模型，⽽且初步結果顯⽰整體成本⼤幅降低了
95% 。
第⼆個案例則是連 NVIDIA ⾃⼰都在⽤。
NVIDIA 透過 Palantir 評估並重新訓練（ post-training ）⾃家的開放模型，結果成
功讓旗下體積最⼩、效率最⾼的模型，在表現上⼤幅超越了⾃家最⼤的模型。
企業在⽇常營運中，往往更需要⼀個真正懂內部業務、且成本合理的專屬
模型，勝過⼀味追求最昂貴卻不懂公司流程的通⽤⼤模型。
舉個簡單的例⼦，⼀家製造商如果要⽤ AI 判斷哪些零件該優先採購，它真正需
要的是模型能讀懂公司的供應商名單、⽣產排程、庫存限制和交貨承諾，並根
據最新狀況隨時調整。
Palantir 想掌握的，正是把底層模型轉化為實際⼯作流程的這⼀層價值。
當企業發現這套做法真的能解決問題，從試點轉為正式⼤單的速度就相當快。

### Page 5

這次財報中， Palantir 公布了四個在⼀兩季內從⼩型試點迅速擴張成⼤型合約的
案例，擴張後的年化合約價值（ ACV ）合計達到 7,650 萬美元：
家電製造商： 從 100 萬美元的初始試點，擴張⾄ 1,500 萬美元的年化合
約。
⼯業材料公司： 從近 300 萬美元試點，擴張⾄ 2,900 萬美元。
全球運動品牌： 從 160 萬美元試點，擴張⾄約 2,100 萬美元。
服飾企業： 透過 AgentCamp 啟動 250 萬美元試點，隨後擴張⾄ 1,150 萬美
元。
其中，那家家電製造商的變化特別有代表性。
他們在第⼆季才剛花 100 萬美元試⽔溫，很快就轉成 300 萬美元的合約；結果
到了第三季，合約⾦額⼜⼀⼝氣放⼤五倍來到 1,500 萬美元，使⽤範圍直接橫
跨銷售（商務）、財務與供應鏈三⼤部⾨。

### Page 6

這代表客戶⼀旦把單⼀功能跑順了，就會開始把不同部⾨的⼯作流程全部串進
同⼀個平台，預算規模也跟著翻倍跳升。
重點⼆：美國商業營收⾸度超⾞政府 —— ⽼客戶越買越多，撐起
第⼆顆成⻑引擎
當單⼀客戶的合約⾦額從⼀百萬變成上千萬美元，反映在全公司的總體財報
上，就促成了這次最具指標性的轉折：
Palantir 的美國商業營收，歷史上第⼀次超越了美國政府營收。
我們可以看看第三季美國市場這兩⼤業務的表現：
美國商業客戶營收：達到  9.84 億美元，年增  148%（季增  29% ）。
美國政府客戶營收：達到  9.53 億美元，年增  96% 。

### Page 7

如果把海外市場也加進來，整體商業總營收來到  11.7 億美元，同樣在歷史上⾸
度超越整體政府營收的  11.5 億美元。
⽐起破紀錄的成⻑率，營收組合的翻轉其實更具代表性。
過去市場對  Palantir 的印象，多半停留在政府、國防與情報系統的承包商；但
隨著商業營收全⾯超⾞，華爾街在它⾝上套了⼗年的「政府承包商」標籤，在
這⼀季正式失效。
過去市場對 Palantir 的印象，多半停留在政府、國防與情報系統的承包商。這類
業務雖然⾨檻極⾼，但容易受到政府預算審查與採購週期的影響。
值得注意的是，這次商業營收能夠超⾞，背後依然伴隨著極為強勁的政府業
務。
美國政府營收年增 96% ，幾乎翻倍；純粹是因為美國企業客戶導⼊的速度衝得
更快，讓公司成功⻑出了第⼆顆規模相當、⽽且爆發⼒更強的成⻑引擎。
如果再往下⼀層看，會有⼀個⽐營收交叉更有意思的發現。

### Page 8

第三季美國商業客戶的總數來到 686 家，年增 29% ；但同期美國商業營收卻⼤
增了 148% 。為什麼營收成⻑的速度，會遠遠甩開客戶數量增加的速度？
答案就在於「淨收⼊留存率（ NDR ）」。這⼀季 Palantir 的整體淨收⼊留存率，
從上⼀季的 157% 進⼀步攀升到 165% 。
這說明了推動營收翻倍的主⼒，很⼤部分來⾃既有客戶持續加碼。
就像前⾯提到的製造商⼀樣，⼀家企業可能先⽤ Palantir 管庫存，覺得好⽤之
後，接著把採購、排程、財務到 AI 決策全部放進來。
不過，這波企業 AI 變現潮⽬前還是⾼度集中在美國。

### Page 9

相較於美國商業營收 148% 的⾼成⻑，第三季國際商業營收只有 1.84 億美元，
年增 21% ，季增更只有 1% 。
這意味著美國企業對軟體轉型的接受度依然領先全球，⽽未來能否把美國驗證
成功的模式複製到海外市場，將是後續觀察的重點。
重點三：營收翻倍但員⼯數幾乎沒變 —— ⾼營業槓桿背後的結構
與代價
把前⾯兩件事串起來看：當客戶透過⼯作坊體驗產品後迅速擴⼤採⽤，⽽且導
⼊之後⼜會⾃⼰越買越多（ NDR 165% ），這會對 Palantir 的成本結構帶來什麼
影響？
執⾏⻑ Alex Karp 在接受媒體（ MarketWatch ）訪問時特別提到了⼀個營運細
節：
Palantir 在美國商業營收⼤增 148% 的同時，全公司的員⼯總數（ Headcount ）
幾乎維持持平。

### Page 10

在傳統的企業軟體產業裡，公司如果想把營收規模擴⼤⼀倍，通常得按⽐例招
募⼤量的業務代表、售前顧問與⽀援⼯程師，⼈⼒成本也會跟著⽔漲船⾼。
但 Karp 形容 Palantir 的成⻑基本上不太依賴傳統意義上的業務員。對他們來說，
產品本⾝（ Foundry 、 Ontology 加上 AIP ）在⼯作坊裡的實際表現，就是最好的
銷售⼯具。
更重要的是，因為底層的資料整合與基礎架構在初期導⼊時就已經建好了，當
客戶後續決定把合約從 300 萬美元擴⼤到 1,500 萬美元時， Palantir 並不需要再投
⼊同等⽐例的⼯程⼈⼒從頭開發。
當營收以接近翻倍的速度成⻑，交付這些服務所需的總⼈數卻沒有等⽐例
增加，這展現了軟體業相當強勁的營業槓桿。
這項特質解釋了為什麼 Palantir 能在⾼成⻑的同時，交出亮眼的獲利數字：
第三季 GAAP 營業利益率達到 47% ，調整後（ Non-GAAP ）營業利益率⾼達
62% ，進⽽推升 Rule of 40 來到 158% 的⽔準。
不過，這種⾼營業槓桿背後也有相對應的代價。

雖然公司的總員⼯數幾乎持平，但為了留住頂尖⼈才並獎勵績效，第三季的股
權激勵費⽤（ SBC ）從去年同期的 1.72 億美元⼤幅增加到 3.19 億美元，幾乎翻
了⼀倍。
這也正是  GAAP 營業利益率（ 47% ）與調整後營業利益率（ 62% ）之間相差 15
個百分點的主要原因。
重點四：拆解 TCV 放緩與 RPO 持平的真相

### Page 11

這份完美財報裡唯⼀的裂縫，在於反映未來幾年營收可⾒度的單季「新簽總合
約價值（ TCV ）」與「未履約義務（ RPO ）」出現了明顯放緩：
整體  TCV ：第三季為  29.18 億美元，年增率僅  6% （相⽐第⼆季為年增
49% ），⽽且較上⼀季的  33.73 億美元下滑了  13.5% 。
美國商業  TCV ：第三季為  14.63 億美元，年增率降⾄  11% （相⽐第⼆季為
年增  153% ），為  2023 年以來的最低單季增速。
剩餘交易價值（ RDV ）與未履約義務（ RPO ）：單季來看，整體  RDV 季
增僅  2% （美國商業  RDV 季增  4% ，低於上⼀季的  27% ）， RPO 則幾乎持
平。
當季營收⼤增  96% 、新簽訂單卻只成⻑  6%，這兩個數字在財務邏輯上不可能
⻑期並存。
當季營收主要是由既有客戶擴張所推動，⽽新簽約指標的放緩，⾃然讓部分投
資⼈開始擔⼼未來幾年的營收可⾒度。
針對這個現象，財務⻑ David Glazer 與經營團隊在法說會上的解釋是：

### Page 12

許多客戶為了快速導⼊ Sovereign AI 的初期功能、急著讓系統趕快上線，所以
在第三季簽下了好幾筆千萬美元級別、但「合約期限⽐往常更短」的訂單。
管理層同時強調，「 Sovereign AI ⾰命才剛滿⼀季」，並預期隨著客戶使⽤深
⼊，這些合約的年限與規模都會隨時間增加。
管理層的這番解釋，其實也剛好從會計規則上說明了為什麼 RPO 幾乎持平：因
為 Palantir 的 RPO 定義，本來就不計⼊初始期限少於 12 個⽉的短期合約。
如果第三季新簽的  Sovereign AI ⼤單多半屬於⼀年以內的短約，那麼不會反映
在 RPO 上、同時壓低單季 TCV 總額，確實符合財務邏輯。
此外，如果我們把時間拉⻑來看，⻑約存量指標其實沒有單季數字看起來那麼
疲弱：
整體  RDV （剩餘交易價值）： 年增率仍達  55% 。
美國商業  RDV ： 達到  65.2 億美元，年增率⾼達  79% 。
過去  12 個⽉（ TTM ）美國商業  TCV ： 累計達  61.14 億美元，年增

62% 。
換句話說，單季簽約指標放緩，還不等於企業端的需求轉弱。真正的問題在於
估值給出的容錯空間極⼩。
以約  178 美元的股價與  24.03 億股計算， Palantir 的市值約  4,286 億美元；扣除
帳上  104 億美元的現⾦與美國公債後，企業價值（ EV ）約為  4,182 億美元。
對照全年營收指引中值  85.32 億美元，企業價值倍數（ EV/Sales ）⾼達  49 倍；
就算⽤第四季指引年化的  106 億美元來算，也⾼達約  39 倍。
將近  40 到  50 倍的營收定價，隱含市場要求這家公司在跨過百億美元年化營收
之後，未來⼗年還得維持約  50% 以上的年複合成⻑率。
在這麼⾼的⾨檻下，市場沒有太多耐⼼等待。
下⼀季投資⼈最想驗證的，就是這批短約能不能如管理層所說，順利續約成更
⻑、更⼤的多年期合約。
從更⼤的產業趨勢來看，企業 AI 的投資重⼼正在轉移。
第⼀波浪潮，⼤家搶著買 GPU 、買算⼒、買⼤模型 API ；但進⼊第⼆階段，企
業真正關⼼的是如何結合內部資料與⼯作流程，讓 AI 實際改善營運並省下成

本。
Palantir 已經在美國市場證明⾃⼰能吃到這波轉型的紅利。

### Page 13

接下來決定它⻑期價值的關鍵，就在於這批新導⼊的短期合約能否在未來幾季
轉化為⻑約存量，並將這套商業模式進⼀步複製到更廣⼤的海外市場。
蘋果：營收創紀錄、⽑利被記憶體成本
壓縮， Service 令⼈失望？
Apple 這次財報，表⾯上相當不錯。
第四季營收來到 1,145 億美元，年增 12% ；每股盈餘 2.10 美元，年增 14% 。主
⼒產品 iPhone 營收成⻑ 17% ， Mac 更是⼤幅成⻑ 24% 。

### Page 14

但在這些亮眼數字背後，如果仔細拆解財報細節與管理層在法說會上的說法，
會發現幾個值得留意的變化。
⾸先是⽑利率。
這⼀季表⾯上 47.9% 的⽑利率，其實包含了約 1 個百分點的關稅退款⼀次性收
益（每股盈餘 2.10 美元裡，也有 0.05 美元來⾃同⼀筆退款）。

### Page 15

扣掉這筆⼀次性收益後，本業⽑利率⼤約是 46.9% ；但就算⽤這個基準來⽐，
Apple 給出的下⼀季（ 12 ⽉季度）⽑利率指引落在 45% ⾄ 46% ，依然在往下⾛。
要知道， 12 ⽉季度是 Apple 傳統的銷售最旺季，營收規模放⼤通常會推升⽑利
率，為什麼這次反⽽逆勢下滑？
財務⻑ Kevan Parekh 在法說會上給出了答案：下⼀季⽑利率下滑，「主要」反映
記憶體成本上升，「另外也包括 Siri AI 上線的部分成本」。
這句話其實就是貫穿整份財報的核⼼線索：全球 AI 浪潮正同時從「硬體成
本」與「軟體商業模式」兩端夾擊 Apple 。
AI 確實帶來了新的成⻑機會，但也正在改變 Apple 原本最賺錢的獲利結構。
我們可以把這次財報拆解成五個環環相扣的重點來看：
重點⼀：硬體的成本逆⾵ —— 全球 AI 算⼒戰，為什麼由 Apple
先買單？

### Page 16

下⼀季⽑利率下滑的最⼤推⼿，來⾃記憶體成本的飆升。 這背後反映了⼀個很
現實的產業現象： Apple 雖然想靠 AI 賺錢，但現在卻得先為全球的 AI 資料中⼼
基建買單。
CEO John Ternus 在法說會上直接⽤「前所未⾒（ unprecedented ）」來形容當
前的記憶體價格環境。
其實在 9 ⽉這⼀季， Apple 就已經感受到記憶體漲價的壓⼒，只是當時⼿裡還有
早先囤積的低價庫存，加上其他⾮記憶體零組件成本下降，才抵銷掉部分衝
擊。
但隨著低價庫存逐漸消耗，這股成本壓⼒將在 12 ⽉季度正式浮現；管理層也坦
⾔，在 12 ⽉季度之後，記憶體採購價格預計仍會持續上升，⽽研調機構更普遍
預期這波供應緊俏會⼀路延續到 2027 年。
為了應對這場結構性的缺貨， Apple 已經採取了兩個動作：
直接漲價： 6 ⽉底先調漲了 Mac 與 iPad 的價格， 9 ⽉⼜將 iPhone 售價調⾼
約 100 美元，且管理層並未排除未來進⼀步漲價的可能。

### Page 17

砸錢鎖定產能： Apple 的庫存⾦額單季⼤增 31% 、年增⾼達 154% ，來到約
145 億美元；從 6 ⽉到 9 ⽉季度，製造採購承諾⾦額更是⼀⼝氣暴增了 330
億美元。
憑藉龐⼤的現⾦流與規模， Apple 搶貨的能⼒絕對⽐中⼩型 Android ⼿機廠或 PC
廠強得多。
但記憶體終究是每⼀台硬體裝置中無法妥協的核⼼成本，當漲價轉嫁給消費者
到了⼀定極限、開始有拉⻑換機週期的⾵險時，硬體⽑利率勢必得承受壓⼒。
全球掀起 AI 資料中⼼擴建潮，最先賺到錢的是記憶體⼤廠，⽽包括 Apple
在內的終端硬體商，正在為這場硬體通膨買單。
重點⼆：⾼⽑利新武器卡關 ——iPhone Duo 的產能與初期品管
挑戰

### Page 18

既然記憶體漲價壓縮了硬體⽑利，最⾃然的解法，就是多賣⼀點⾼單價、⾼⽑
利的新產品來拉⾼平均售價（ ASP ）。
這就帶出了市場最關注的新品： Apple ⾸款折疊機 iPhone Duo 。
iPhone Duo 在 9 ⽉底財報結算後才正式上市，因此第四季財報還沒有反映它的
營收。
從需求⾯來看，這款新機⾮常成功，預購迅速秒殺，出貨時間⼀路排到明年⼀
⽉下旬。
但問題出在供給⾯。
這次法說會上， CEO John Ternus 特別安排營運⻑ Sabih Khan ⼀起上線回答供應
鏈問題。
Khan 坦⾔， iPhone Duo ⽬前處於「產能受限」的狀態，且「很難預測供需何
時能達到平衡」。

### Page 19

這種罕⾒不給時間表的說法，意味著折疊螢幕與轉軸的量產爬坡難度，⽐過去
傳統 iPhone ⾼出許多。
更⿇煩的是，初期有極少數⽤戶反映內螢幕轉軸處出現摺痕或裂紋的品質問
題；雖然 Apple 已經在協助換機，但緊繃的產能也讓部分地區的換貨等待時間
拉⻑。
除了折疊機，過去受限於先進製程（隨著產品線轉向 2 奈⽶已逐漸緩解）的
Mac 與 iPad ，到了 12 ⽉季度依然⾯臨缺貨，⽽這次卡住產能的瓶頸，剛好⼜是
前⾯提到的記憶體不⾜。
換句話說，儘管下⼀季營收預計仍能成⻑ 7% ⾄ 10% 、有望創下公司歷史新
⾼，但記憶體漲價加上⾼階新品缺貨，讓硬體業務在短期內很難幫公司撐起⽑
利率。
這時候， Apple 就更需要仰賴⾼⽑利的 Services （服務業務）來穩住獲利基本
盤。
第四季 Services 營收只占公司總營收約 28% ，卻因為⾼達 76% 的⽑利率，貢獻
了全公司將近 44% 的⽑利。

### Page 20

硬體負責把⼈帶進⽣態系， Services 負責賺取⾼利潤，這是 Apple 最核⼼的賺錢
公式。
然⽽，正如財務⻑在⽑利率指引中透露的，連 Services 這座⾼⽑利堡壘，也從
下⼀季開始要⾯對 AI 帶來的成本考驗。
重點三： Siri AI 終於有了收費⽅向，但會不會稀釋 76% 的⾼⽑
利？
Apple 在今年 WWDC 就已經宣布，部分需要雲端模型處理的 AI 功能將設有使
⽤額度，使⽤者未來可以付費取得更多運算容量。
這次法說會上， Goldman Sachs 分析師 Michael Ng 進⼀步追問： iCloud+ 是否正
在轉型成 Apple AI 商業化的重要⼯具？
管理層給出了肯定的答案，確認未來需要更多伺服器端 AI 運算能⼒的使⽤者，
可以付費購買額外容量。
過去⼤家訂閱 iCloud+ ，買的是「儲存空間」，⽤來放照⽚、影⽚和備份檔案。
到了 AI 時代，使⽤者額外付費購買的變成了「運算能⼒」，像是讓 Siri 分析複
雜⽂件、執⾏多次雲端請求，或是呼叫⼤型模型處理⼯作。

### Page 21

坐擁超過 25 億台活躍裝置、超過 15 億個付費訂閱的 Apple ，要推這項新服務當
然很有優勢。
假設未來 Apple 能吸引 5,000 萬⼈、每⽉額外付 10 美元使⽤進階 AI 功能，⼀年
就能帶來 60 億美元的營收，相當於⽬前全年 Services 營收的 4.9% 左右。
但從財務⾓度來看，這⾨⽣意跟過去的數位服務有⼀個本質上的差異。
傳統數位服務的營收增加，不太需要同⽐例增加成本；但⼤型 AI 模型每次執⾏
雲端推論，背後都在消耗真實的算⼒資源。使⽤者越頻繁使⽤複雜的 AI 功能，
Apple 要承擔的伺服器與推論成本就越⾼。

### Page 22

過去每收到 100 美元服務收⼊， Apple 可以留下 76 美元⽑利；但到了 AI 服務
時代，營收增加的同時，背後往往伴隨著持續攀升的雲端推論成本。
這也解釋了為什麼財務⻑ Kevan Parekh 特別強調， Siri AI 的雲端推論費⽤將
計⼊ Services 的銷貨成本，⽽且相關成本已經開始反映在 12 ⽉季度的⽑利率指
引中。
同時， Apple 還透露了⼀個會計處理上的改變：當 Siri AI 成為硬體裝置隨附的服
務時，公司會將⼀部分的硬體收⼊分配給這項服務，並在裝置使⽤期間逐步認
列。
這代表就算消費者沒有額外掏錢加購算⼒， Apple 的 Services 營收與成本結構，
也會因為 AI 功能的推出⽽重新洗牌。
因此，接下來觀察 Apple 的 AI 變現能⼒，除了看有多少⼈願意付費，更要看每
增加⼀美元的 AI 收⼊，究竟能留下多少⽑利。
這也是為什麼 Apple 持續投⼊⾃研晶⽚與裝置端 AI ，除了讓⼿機跑得更順，
背後更有明確的財務考量：把越多的運算留在裝置端處理，⻑期下來就能

省下越多的雲端推論成本。

### Page 23

重點四：華爾街真正擔⼼的事 —— 當 AI Agent 成為⼊⼝，
Apple 還能收多少過路費？
如果說推論成本影響的是「利潤率」，那 Morgan Stanley 和 Evercore 分析師在
法說會上追問的，則是關係到「營收來源」的根本問題：
當 AI 從聊天機器⼈演變成能替使⽤者執⾏任務的 Agent （代理），會不會威脅
到 Apple 現有的 Services 業務？
我們可以想像⼀個很⽇常的場景。
現在如果你想規劃⼀趟旅⾏，通常會先打開 Google 搜尋機票，進到航空公司網
站⽐價，接著打開訂房 App 找飯店，最後分別完成付款。整個過程必須切換好
幾個不同的平台與 App 。
但在不久的將來，你可能只需要對⼿機裡的 AI Agent 說⼀句：「幫我安排下週末
去東京的旅⾏，機票加飯店預算三萬台幣，飯店希望離⾞站近⼀點。」
接下來， AI Agent 就會⾃動幫你搜尋、篩選，並直接串接不同服務完成預訂。
從頭到尾，你可能連訂房 App 都沒有打開過。
這個轉變最核⼼的影響，在於「誰掌握了決定權」。 過去是使⽤者決定要打開

哪個 App ；未來則是 AI Agent 決定要調⽤哪⼀家服務商。

### Page 24

⼀旦消費者習慣把⽇常決策交給 ChatGPT 、 Gemini 或其他第三⽅ AI Agent ，
Apple 過去靠 iOS 與 App Store 築起的收費關卡就會⾯臨兩個挑戰：
App Store 分潤壓⼒： 如果第三⽅  Agent 直接透過網⾴、外部  API 或其他
⽀付管道完成交易， Apple 對數位商品與訂閱交易的抽成能⼒就會被繞
過。
搜尋授權收⼊重分配： Apple 每年從搜尋引擎拿到的巨額授權⾦，建⽴在
「 iPhone 是最重要搜尋⼊⼝」的商業價值上。當  AI Agent 逐漸取代傳統
搜尋與  App 操作，這個⼊⼝的經濟價值勢必會重新洗牌。

### Page 25

⾯對這個問題， John Ternus 的回答展現了 Apple 的防守策略。
他強調， iPhone 是使⽤者最重要的個⼈資訊中⼼，裡⾯存放著聯絡⼈、訊息、
照⽚、⾏事曆、健康與財務資料。
AI 如果想真正理解使⽤者需求並跨 App 完成任務，就必須取得這些個⼈脈絡與
系統權限。
這讓未來的 AI Agent 競爭，分成了兩種不同的控制權：
需求⼊⼝： 誰最懂使⽤者想做什麼，誰來決定推薦哪些商品與服務（這
是  OpenAI 、 Google 正在搶的）。
執⾏⼊⼝： 誰能安全地讀取個⼈資料、呼叫  App 功能，並完成最後的操
作（這是  Apple 透過  iOS 、 App Intents 與  Siri AI 牢牢握在⼿裡的）。

### Page 26

Apple 很清楚⾃⼰未必要做出全世界最龐⼤的模型，但它必須利⽤作業系統與
隱私權限的優勢，把「需求」與「執⾏」整合在⾃⼰的平台上，確保使⽤者依
然透過 Siri 來完成⽇常任務。
⽽這場⼊⼝保衛戰之所以迫在眉睫，從這次 Services 的財測就能看出端倪。
Apple 第四季 Services 營收達到創紀錄的 315.7 億美元，年增約 10% ；但下⼀季
的成⻑指引卻放緩到 8% ⾄ 9% 。
管理層解釋，放緩的主因來⾃「第三⽅授權收⼊」與「匯率」變化，其次則是
部分 App Store 與⼿遊業務的調整。

### Page 27

值得注意的是，管理層⼝中的「第三⽅授權收⼊」，背後很⼤⼀部分就是
Google ⽀付給 Apple 的搜尋引擎分潤。
雖然單季度的波動未必完全來⾃ AI Agent 的衝擊，但這正好印證了華爾街的擔
憂：只要搜尋授權與 App Store 這兩⼤⽀柱稍微放緩， Services 的成⻑動能就會
⽴刻感受到壓⼒。
重點五：現在的股價，到底在賭什麼？
把前⾯硬體與軟體的拼圖全部放在⼀起，我們最後來看估值。
以財報當⽇收盤價約 338 美元、以及 FY2026 實際每股盈餘 8.98 美元計算，
Apple 的歷史本益⽐約為 37.7 倍（財報公布後盤後股價回落⾄約 329 美元，換算
也⾼達 36.6 倍）。

### Page 28

37.7 倍的本益⽐聽起來很貴，但「貴」是⼀個很模糊的詞。換個問法會清楚很
多：如果今天⽤ 338 美元買進 Apple ，然後⼀直抱著不賣，你每年⼤概能賺多
少？
有⼀個很簡單的算法：
⻑期年報酬  ≈ 現⾦殖利率  + ⻑期成⻑率
現⾦殖利率，就是公司⼀年賺進的⾃由現⾦流，除以你付出的市值。
Apple 現在市值接近 5 兆美元，⼀年的⾃由現⾦流⼤約只有市值的 2.6% 。
換句話說，⽤ 338 美元買進，第⼀年真正拿回的現⾦回報只有 2.6% 左右，剩下
的報酬全靠未來成⻑。
⽤這個邏輯反推，可以從兩個⾓度看現在的股價：
如果假設  Apple ⻑期每年成⻑  3% ：
338 美元的股價，換算下來的⻑期年報酬⼤約是  6.1% （註：若以純現⾦殖利率
2.6% 直接相加則約為  5.6% ）。

### Page 29

同⼀時間，美國⼗年期公債殖利率約  4.9% ，⽽且幾乎沒有⾵險。
這代表買  Apple ⽐買公債多承擔了股價波動的⾵險，每年卻只多換來約  1 個百
分點左右的⾵險補償；⽽在歷史上，美股投資⼈要求的⾵險補償通常落在  4%
到  5% 。
如果投資⼈要求每年  7% 的報酬：
這只⽐公債多  2 個百分點左右，已經算很客氣了。
但要做到這樣， Apple 必須永遠每年成⻑約  3.6% 以上。
⼀般估值模型給成熟公司的永續成⻑率，多半抓在  2% 到  3% ；要求  3.6% ，等
於要求⼀家市值近  5 兆美元的巨頭，永遠跟著整個美國經濟的速度⼀起⻑⼤。
這兩個⾓度講的是同⼀件事：現在的股價，幾乎沒有留空間給意外。
這時候再回頭看⽑利率，就能理解市場為什麼對那 1 到 2 個百分點的變化這麼
敏感。

### Page 30

以 2026 財年全年 Services ⾼達 1,233 億美元的營收規模來算，服務⽑利率只要
每下降 1 個百分點，⼀年就少掉約 12.3 億美元的⽑利。
若放⼤到全公司約 4,790 億美元的總營收來看，全公司⽑利率每少 1 個百分點，
扣稅後⼤約會少掉每股 0.27 美元的盈餘；⽤ 37.7 倍的本益⽐換算，等於股價直
接少了約 10 美元。
更別忘了，連計算 37.7 倍本益⽐所⽤的 8.98 美元 EPS 起點，裡⾯都還包含了⼀
筆關稅退款的⼀次性收益。
所以這次財報最值得思考的地⽅，在於現在的股價，其實已經先預設了 Apple
會有個完美結局：
市場預設記憶體漲價很快會過去、 Siri AI 的雲端算⼒成本控制得住，⽽且第三
⽅ AI Agent 也搶不⾛ iPhone 的流量⼊⼝。
對 Apple 來說，投⼊ AI 同時是⼀項成⻑投資，也是⼀筆為了保護每年 1,233 億
美元服務⽣意的必要⽀出。
只要硬體缺料漲價、 AI 推論成本攀升、或是⼊⼝分流這三件事當中有任何⼀件

拖得⽐預期更久，市場除了要下修獲利預估，連那 37.7 倍的估值倍數本⾝都可
能⾯臨重新定價。
⽽在現在這個價位，後者帶來的影響往往更⼤。

## Document 426: Solvency II reforms offer limited support for bank bonds, but securitisation gains some ground

Source PDF: all report pdf/Solvency II reforms offer limited support for bank bonds, but securitisation gains some ground.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 11
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The Solvency II amendments are unlikely to result in a material
increase in demand for bank bonds. Securitisations beneﬁt
most directly through lower capital charges, while covered
bonds could beneﬁt modestly from the new liquidity-buﬀer
rules
Maureen Schuller
On 30 January 2027, the amendments to the Solvency II regime
will apply, making these revisions one of the important regulatory
themes for 2027. One of the key objectives of the Solvency II

reforms is to release capital and promote long-term investments by
European insurance companies in support of the ambitions of
Europe’s Savings and Investments Union (SIU). Such capital release
could also increase the capacity of insurance companies to invest
more in corporate and bank bonds.
During the past decade, insurance companies have reduced their
bond holdings in favour of equity. Rather than an active
reallocation of assets, this partially reﬂects second-round eﬀects

related to the strong equity market growth and the rise in bond

have also coincided in recent years with a stronger focus by banks
on issuance in shorter maturities, rather than the longer maturities

We don’t expect the Solvency II revisions to reverse this trend,
particularly as the amendments promote long-term equity
investments. Instead, for most bank bonds, the spread-risk capital
treatment remains broadly unchanged. Unsecured and
subordinated bank bonds continue to be treated as corporate

bonds with capital charges determined by their ratings and
duration, while qualifying covered bonds keep their preferential
treatment. The impact of the Solvency II revisions therefore arises
mainly indirectly through lower overall capital requirements, while
securitisations are the only debt instruments beneﬁting directly
through a more favourable capital treatment.
Nonetheless, certain reforms are worth highlighting for their
potential impact on insurance demand for diﬀerent bank bonds.

Among these, the amendments relating to long-term equity

### Page 3

investments, securitisations and the volatility adjustment are

Long-term equity investments and non-
life liquidity buffers
Under Solvency II, insurance companies can classify part of their
equity holdings as long-term equity investments (LTEI), beneﬁting
from a lower 22% capital charge. To qualify, these investments
must be held for at least ﬁve years, be listed in the EEA or OECD,
meet diversiﬁcation standards and be managed under a long-term

investment policy. The insurer must also be able to avoid forced
sales for ﬁve years, even under stressed conditions.
The Solvency II amended Delegated Regulation introduces two
approaches to demonstrate that forced equity sales can be
avoided. 1) The insurer must either have suﬃcient illiquid
homogeneous life insurance liabilities with a duration of more than
9.5 years or a liquidity buﬀer exceeding 100% for non-life insurance
obligations. 2) The insurer can also apply a forced-selling test to

prove that it can generate cash inﬂows that are consistently higher
than the cash outﬂows over ﬁve years, including under stress.
The liquidity buﬀer requirements for non-life insurance activities
are similar to the Liquidity Coverage Ratio (LCR) requirements for
banks. The portfolio of liquid assets can comprise Level 1, Level 2A
and Level 2B assets, with the total value of Level 2A and Level 2B
assets capped at 40% and the value of the Level 2B assets at 15%.

Qualifying Level 1 assets, subject to a 0% haircut, are cash,
exposures to the ECB, multilateral development banks and
international organisations, and domestic currency exposures
to EEA central governments and central banks.
Qualifying Level 2A assets, subject to a 15% haircut, are bonds
and loans rated AA- or better that are not issued by an
insurance company or bank, and covered bonds rated AA- or
better as referred to in Article 3(1) of the European Covered

Bond Directive (CBD) (ie ‘European Covered Bonds’).
Qualifying Level 2B assets, subject to a 25% haircut, are
Simple, Transparent and Standardised (STS) securitisations (not

While unsecured bank bonds are excluded as eligible liquid assets,

the LTEI-related liquidity buﬀer approach could generate some
additional demand for covered bonds or STS securitisations of
banks. However, we don’t expect this eﬀect to be signiﬁcant given
the relatively small share of non-life insurance obligations and
insurers’ sizeable existing portfolios of other liquid assets, such as
government bonds.

Lower capital requirements for
securitisations

### Page 5

The amended Solvency II Delegated Regulation lowers the capital
requirements for securitisation notes. This should encourage
insurers to invest more in these transactions, helping banks to
transfer risk and expand lending.
The risk factors (a and b ) for the spread risk of senior tranches
of STS securitisations will become fully aligned with the
treatment of similarly rated covered bonds as referred to in CBD
Article 3(1).
The risk factors for non-senior STS securitisations are reduced

to a similar extent, with the new capital charges for AAA-rated
non-senior notes at c.70% of the current capital charge, and for
AA-rated non-senior notes at 75% of the current requirement.
The diﬀerence in the capital treatment between STS and non-
STS securitisations is currently far more signiﬁcant for insurers
than for banks. This diﬀerence will be reduced substantially,
while the capital charge remains capped at 100%.
The current Solvency II regime makes no distinction between

senior and non-senior non-STS securitisation notes,
exaggerating the spread risk of senior compared with non-
senior tranches. Hence, lower risk factors are introduced for
senior non-STS compared to non-senior non-STS transactions.
As a result of these amendments, the diﬀerence in capital
requirements between CBD-compliant covered bonds and senior
STS securitisations will disappear from the end of January, while in
the highest rating buckets (AAA and AA) securitisations will be

treated better than unsecured bonds or third-country covered
bonds. Further down the rating scale, STS securitisations will be
treated closer to that of unsecured bonds from a capital
perspective, where corporate and bank unsecured bonds will still
beneﬁt from a more favourable capital charge than similarly rated
senior STS securitisations.
Securitisation alternatives will become
more competitive from a capital perspective

### Page 6

Source: European Commission, ING
European insurance companies currently hold only about €37bn in
collateralised debt, such as asset-backed securities (ABS) and
residential mortgage-backed securities (RMBS). This is only 12% of
what they hold in covered bonds and 3% of their corporate debt
portfolio.
The Solvency II amendments may motivate insurance companies
to diversify their debt portfolios a bit further away from covered
bonds and other bank bond products towards securitisations.

However, the impact is not expected to be substantial given that
the weighted average lives of securitisation notes may not always
be well suited to the longer-term investment needs of insurance
companies.
Volatility adjustment to align spread
sensitivities more closely
Insurance companies calculate the best estimate of their liabilities
by discounting the expected future liability cash ﬂows using the
relevant risk-free interest rate term structure. To mitigate the

balance sheet impact of bond spread volatility (ie, on the asset side
of their balance sheet), insurance companies can discount their
liabilities at the risk-free rate plus a volatility adjustment (VA).
The volatility adjustment increases the
discount rate

### Page 7

Source: EIOPA, ING
For each relevant currency (or national market in the case of the
euro area), the volatility adjustment represents 65% of the spread
(S) between the interest rate on the reference portfolio and the
relevant risk-free interest rate, adjusted for a risk correction (RC). The
latter correction factor removes the part of the spread that is
attributable to expected losses or unexpected credit or other risks
to the assets. The volatility adjustment is only added to the

applicable risk-free interest rates that are not derived from
extrapolation.
For each relevant country, a country-speciﬁc adjustment may also
be added to the volatility adjustment before the application of the
65% factor if a) the risk-corrected country spread is more than twice
the risk-corrected currency spread, and b) the risk-corrected country
spread is more than 85bp. The country component in the volatility
adjustment helps mitigate excessive widening in speciﬁc countries.

Under the current regime, the representative currency and country
portfolios are constructed as uniform portfolios, even though
insurers’ actual ﬁxed-income portfolios may diﬀer substantially.
Some insurers can therefore beneﬁt excessively from the volatility
adjustment if the resulting reduction in the best estimate of their
liabilities exceeds the decline in the value of the relevant assets
when credit spreads widen.

### Page 8

For this reason, the Solvency II revisions change the calculation of
the volatility adjustment by a) making the applicable risk
corrections (RC) more spread-sensitive, and b) introducing a credit
spread sensitivity ratio (CSSR) to better align the volatility
adjustment with the insurer’s asset-liability proﬁle. The latter ratio
reﬂects the price value of a basis point of the insurer’s bond,
securitisation and loan investments versus the price value of a

basis point of the best estimate of liabilities, with a minimum of 0
and a maximum of 1.
Moreover, subject to approval by the supervisory authority, an
undertaking-speciﬁc adjustment (USA) of up to 105% may be
applied to align the risk-corrected spread on the reference portfolio
more closely with that of the insurer’s own portfolio. This must not
lead to an increase in the applied risk-adjusted spread for more
than two consecutive quarters. Moreover, when the USA is used, the

insurer cannot also increase the volatility adjustment through a
macro volatility adjustment.
The macro volatility adjustment replaces the existing country-
speciﬁc adjustment and can be added to the euro volatility
adjustment when the relevant conditions are met. It aims to
mitigate the impact of excessive spread widening in individual euro
area countries and is based on the diﬀerence between the risk-
corrected euro spread and the risk-corrected spread for the relevant

country.
Volatility adjustment calculations

### Page 9

Subscripts cu = currency, co = country, ma = macro; VA = volatility
adjustment, S = spread of the reference portfolio, RC = risk correction, RCS
= risk-corrected spread of the reference portfolio, CSSR = credit spread
sensitivity ratio, USA = undertaking-speciﬁc adjustment.
Source: European Commission, ING
The introduction of additional minimum conditions, such as
supervisory approval and the availability of adequate processes to
calculate the volatility adjustment, has also facilitated the upward

revision of the general application ratio (GAR) from 65% to 85%.
Primarily through the introduction of the credit spread sensitivity
ratio (CSSR), the volatility adjustment will better reﬂect the
relationship between each insurer’s asset and liability sensitivity to
changes in the volatility adjustment. The revision may provide
modest support for bonds that increase the asset portfolio’s spread
sensitivity, as a higher CSSR results in a larger volatility adjustment

until the ratio reaches its cap of one. The revised calculations should
also reduce the possibility for some insurers to beneﬁt
disproportionately from the volatility adjustment when their assets
are less spread-sensitive than the reference portfolio.
Conclusion
The Solvency II amendments are unlikely to trigger a material
reallocation in favour of bank bonds. Securitisations are the most
obvious beneﬁciaries of a more favourable capital treatment, while

covered bonds and unsecured bank bonds should mainly beneﬁt
indirectly from lower overall capital requirements for insurers. We

## Document 431: Taiwan Earnings Call Weekly Recap - Is Earnings Still Enough to Drive Stock Price_ _ GSTW

Source PDF: all report pdf/Taiwan Earnings Call Weekly Recap - Is Earnings Still Enough to Drive Stock Price_ _ GSTW.pdf

### Page 1

Taiwan Earnings Call Weekly Recap - Is Earnings
Still Enough to Drive Stock Price? | GSTW
30 October 2026 | 4:17 PM Taiwan Standard Time
Robert Wang
Goldman Sachs (Asia) L.L.C. Taipei Branch
FICC & Equities
Short week but busy for Taiwan in terms of earnings. Index is only down 1.8% WTD,
though multiple large caps saw over 10% drawdown in this week alone. The elevated
level in Taiwan vs other regional markets still attracted much short interest without

much buying interest except from retail. Overall sentiment is not bearish, though
investors have turned much more defensive and hiding in sectors such as Financials,
Telecom, and TSMC.
For earnings, winners' momentum was short lived; instead of chasing into the beat,
many chose to further profit take on the strength (Unimicron, EMC/TUC, Landmark
on open right after their earnings). Companies that missed the results were definitely
punished (i.e. Chroma, Delta, UMC). Echoing back to my previous note, range bound

trade seems to be the safest way to stay afloat.
See below for my single stock takeaway:
Mediatek (2454 TT)
Financial Highlights:
3Q26 revenue reached NT$168.06bn (+10% QoQ and +18% YoY) with EPS at
NT$17.5 (+15% QoQ and +11% YoY) vs cons. NT$15.95. GM came in at 46.3%
(+11ppt QoQ and 18ppt YoY) vs cons. 46.2%.
3Q26 outperformance was mainly from stronger-than-expected customer
demand and improved supply chain yield. First ASIC already started shipping since

Sep'26.
Operational Outlook:
Revenue outlook for 4Q26 is NT$210 - NT$222bn, translating to a 29% sequential
growth. 2027 revenue growth will likely grow by high single digits yoy. GM will be
around 45% with +/-1.5%, and OPM is expected to be at 26% +/-2%.
ASIC chip sales for 2026 will be around US$2.5bn, higher than previous guidance,
and it will reach US$16bn in 2027, around 20% of ASIC TAM and at the higher end
of the company guidance. Next gen chip's tape out is on track and targeted to

enter mass production in 1Q28.
Delta Electronics (2308 TT)
10/30/26, 4:46 PM Taiwan Earnings Call Weekly Recap - Is Earnings Still Enough to Drive Stock Price? | GSTW
https://marquee.gs.com/s/content/markets/2971c821-c2cc-403a-acfe-2b906dd125c4 1/11

### Page 2

Financial Highlights:
3Q26 revenue reached NT$197.1bn (+8% QoQ / +31% YoY), while EPS registered
at NT$9.05. GM declined to 34.1% (down 1.5ppt QoQ), impacted by a NT$3.9bn
inventory write-down (~1.7% of revenue) mostly in Power Electronics.
Operating profit rose +7% QoQ (+31% YoY) backed by operating leverage, keeping
OPM resilient at 16.6% flat QoQ. Net profit was NT$23.5bn (-7% QoQ / +26% YoY)
due to lower non-operating investment gains.
Operational Outlook:

Management expects 4Q26 revenue to grow sequentially with gross margins
unlikely to deteriorate, aided by inventory normalization and potential price
adjustments to offset ~2% YTD material cost inflation.
AI-related exposure approaches 50% of total revenue (liquid cooling at ~13%), and
management remains confident in AI infrastructure demand through 2030 despite
near-term bottlenecks.
Key Q&A:
Inventory Write-down & Normalization: The NT$3.9bn write-down stemmed from

component mismatches and slower customer ramp-ups of newer platforms (e.g.,
18.5kW vs. 5.5kW platforms); management expects affected inventory to be
progressively consumed by 1Q27.
HVDC Adoption & Vicor Litigation: While 400V products saw partial shipments in
3Q26, 800V adoption is slower and primarily NVIDIA-driven. On the Vicor VPD
patent dispute, management expects no near-term impact on customer
relationships or shipments, with an initial ITC determination unlikely until 2027.

ASE Technology Holding (3711 TT)
Financial Highlights:
3Q26 consolidated revenue reached NT$244.16bn, with a GM of 22.1% and EPS of
NT$5.84, all outperforming market expectations.
ATM (Advanced Semiconductor Engineering, Test and Materials) GM hit 30.6%,
breaking past previous structural highs; the company announced 30% as its
baseline gross margin for the future.
Operational Outlook:
4Q26 consolidated revenue is projected to grow 6%–8% QoQ, with GM between

23.0%–24.0% and OPM between 14.5%–15.5%.
CPO is set to enter mass production in 4Q26, with related revenue expected to
contribute over US$500mn in 2027; the full-year LEAP revenue target has been
revised upward to over US$4bn.
Key Q&A:
Capex Upward Revision: Due to strong demand for LEAP and mainstream
advanced packaging, full-year 2026 capex was raised from US$10.5bn to
US$11.5bn (60% allocated to LEAP, 40% to mainstream packaging), with 2027
capex expected to be even higher.

Capacity Bottlenecks & Expansion: Factory floor space is currently the primary
constraint on near-term growth. The company is actively searching for and
acquiring existing facilities to shorten setup timelines, prioritizing resource
allocation toward packaging and wafer sort.
Elite Material (2383 TT)
10/30/26, 4:46 PM Taiwan Earnings Call Weekly Recap - Is Earnings Still Enough to Drive Stock Price? | GSTW
https://marquee.gs.com/s/content/markets/2971c821-c2cc-403a-acfe-2b906dd125c4 2/11

### Page 3

Financial Highlights:
Driven by strong demand, actual 3Q26 revenue grew 29% QoQ, comfortably
beating prior conservative expectations.
GM outperformed expectations, hitting 37.7% for the quarter.
Operational Outlook:
4Q26 revenue growth is conservatively estimated to be at least 15%–20% QoQ,
with gross margins expanding further to 38.0%–39.5%.
Proactive capacity expansion plans (targeting 15.25 million sheets/month by end-
2030 vs. 5.85 million sheets/month currently) are still insufficient to support all

signed Long-Term Agreements (LTAs) for the next 4 years, pointing to a severe
supply-demand gap and zero oversupply risk.
Key Q&A:
CCL Pricing & Substrate Expansion: EMC is executing its second round of high-end
CCL (M7+) price hikes this year (5%–20% increases covering M7/M8 and M8+/M9
grades). Furthermore, it is rapidly expanding high-end ABF substrate core-layer
CCL capacity from 300k sheets/month to at least 1.2 million sheets/month by end-
2027, targeting commercial shipments starting in 4Q27.

Competition & Market Share: While AI customers are qualifying second or third
sources to secure supply chains, EMC maintains its long-term target of holding a
50% AI CCL market share, supported by best-in-class product quality and deep
CSP/PCB collaboration.
Yageo Corporation (2327 TT)
Financial Highlights:
3Q26 revenue reached NT$49.53bn (+11.4% QoQ, +49.7% YoY), with a GM of 41%
and EPS of NT$5.83.
Free cash flow surged to NT$9.236 billion, while the net financial debt-to-EBITDA

ratio dropped from 59% in 4Q25 to 24% in 3Q26, reflecting a continuously
improving financial structure and robust cash generation.
Operational Outlook:
4Q26 revenue, GM, and OPM are all expected to edge up sequentially, defying
typical seasonal flat-to-down trends for the fourth quarter.
AI demand remains solid, with its contribution to total revenue expanding from
15% in 1Q26 and 16% in 2Q26 to approximately 20% in 3Q26.
Key Q&A:
Capacity & Expansion Plans: Capacity utilization rates stood at 95% in 3Q26 and

are expected to exceed 95% in 4Q26, surpassing the healthy range of 85%–90%.
Total capacity for tantalum capacitors and MLCCs is planned to expand by 10%–
15% in 2027, with portions coming online starting in the second quarter.
Order Momentum & B/B Ratio: The overall book-to-bill (B/B) ratio in 3Q26
remained close to 2.0, with AI-related tantalum capacitors and MLCCs seeing
particularly robust orders, while LTAs with core customers continue to underpin

future shipments.
Nan Ya Plastics (1303 TT)
Financial Highlights:
10/30/26, 4:46 PM Taiwan Earnings Call Weekly Recap - Is Earnings Still Enough to Drive Stock Price? | GSTW
https://marquee.gs.com/s/content/markets/2971c821-c2cc-403a-acfe-2b906dd125c4 3/11

### Page 4

3Q26 revenue rose to NT$95.6bn (+14.3% QoQ), with electronic materials
expanding to 58.1% of total sales. Operating profit surged 61.2% QoQ to
NT$18.31bn, of which electronic materials contributed 93.8%.
EPS hit a record NT$4.90, bringing 9M26 EPS to a record NT$10.07, supported by
electronic material profitability and investment income from Nanya Technology.
Operational Outlook:
CCL has surpassed NYPCB as the segment's primary revenue driver (31% of

electronic materials) with segment OPM scaling to 30.9% (up from 19.9% in 2Q26).
Management guided for fiberglass fabrics and CCL price hikes to continue at a 5–
10% MoM increase pace through early 2027, alongside a notable product mix shift
toward M7/M8 in 1Q27.
Key Q&A:
CCL Pricing and M-Series Mix: CCL and fiberglass-cloth pricing remains firm into
early 2027 with monthly 5–10% increases. M-series products reached ~30% of
CCL revenue in September, with management expecting further mix improvement

toward M7/M8 as CSP projects lock in.
Capacity Expansion and M10 Development: Monthly CCL capacity is projected to
expand from 6.18m to 6.48m sheets by year-end. For M9/M10, Nan Ya is
independently formulating hydrocarbon resins and fiberglass incorporating low-
dielectric and quartz materials with solid sampling progress.
TUC (6274 TT)
Financial Highlights:
3Q26 financial results beat market consensus, reporting revenue of NT17.95bn,
GM of 36.9%, operating margin of 31.0%, and EPS of NT$12.88, outperforming

consensus estimates by 29% to 35%.
The earnings beat was driven by continuous growth in high-end product revenue
and a return to profitability for the Thailand plant, which successfully offset earlier
first-half losses.
Operational Outlook:
For 4Q26, high-end product pricing is initiating upward adjustments. With the
Thailand plant adding another 100k sheets of capacity and all series of CCL seeing
price hike opportunities (especially high-end M7/M8 catch-up price increases

alongside tightening mid-to-low-end fiberglass cloth supply), product mix and
profitability are set to improve further.
ASIC AI servers are emerging as a core growth engine, alongside long-term
opportunities from satellite and space data centers. TUC is adding several large
ASIC customers (some already in sampling/board qualification stages) and
expanding its customer base in 400G, 800G, and 1.6T switches.
Key Q&A / Detailed Highlights:
ASIC & Switch Expansion: AI servers will serve as the primary growth driver, with

multiple major ASIC clients entering the board-maker certification stage;
meanwhile, existing 400G switch customers are rapidly accelerating 800G and 1.6T
switch certifications, with some expecting 1.6T demand to outpace 800G by 2027.
Satellite and PTFE Solutions: Space satellite PCBs utilizing M8+ materials are slated
to begin contribution in 1H27, with long-term customer demand forecasts already
extended through 2032. For PTFE, TUC boasts ~5–6 years of R&D foundation

rather than being a newcomer, though external partnerships may be pursued to
bypass special high-temperature press machine capacity limitations

### Page 5

Win Semiconductors (3105 TT)
Financial Highlights:
3Q26 revenue reached NT$5.86bn (+11.5% QoQ / +31% YoY) with capacity
utilization improving to 75%. GM expanded significantly by 9.2ppts QoQ to an 18-
quarter high of 37.4% (standalone GM reached 40.7%), while OPM rose to 24.2%
and EPS reached NT$4.55.
Margin expansion was primarily driven by a favorable product mix shift toward
higher-margin Infrastructure and Optical products rather than utilization gains

alone.
Operational Outlook:
Management guided 4Q26 revenue for low-single-digit QoQ growth—beating
normal smartphone seasonality—with gross margins sustaining in the high-30%
range.
Moving into FY27, photodetector (PD) production is expected to ramp materially,
while CW Laser and EML are anticipated to contribute more meaningfully starting
in FY28[cite: 388].
Key Q&A:
Infrastructure and Satellite Growth: Infrastructure became WIN's largest revenue

contributor at 35–40% in 3Q26, driven by LEO satellites, aerospace, and long-
distance communications; demand is expected to stay robust into FY27.
Optical and PD Production Timeline: Optical revenue rose QoQ on initial PD mass
production; PD will see significantly higher full-year shipment volumes in FY27,
while CW Laser and EML will generate limited initial revenue before a FY28 volume
ramp.

realize each day a loss or a gain which is a multiple (for leveraged ETFs and ETNs) or the inverse (for inverse ETFs and ETNs) of the

performance on that day of their underlying index or benchmark. For this reason, if they are held for a period longer than one day, their
performance over such longer periods of time can differ significantly from the stated multiple of the performance (or the inverse of the
performance) of their underlying index or benchmark during the same period of time. This effect can be magnified in volatile markets.
Prior to entering into a transaction in leveraged or inverse ETFs, you should be aware of the general risks associated with such

transactions. You should not enter into leveraged or inverse ETFs transactions unless you understand the nature and extent of your risk
exposure. You should also be satisfied that the leveraged or inverse ETFs transaction is appropriate for you in light of your circumstances
and financial condition. ETFs and ETNs that are linked to commodity futures do not offer direct exposure to the commodity's spot price,
and may perform differently than the spot price for the commodity itself. Performance differential can be magnified if a specific

condition persists in the market for a commodity that creates a disparity between near-term future prices and long-term future prices
and may lead to unexpected performance results. Other factors, such as roll yield, transaction costs, management fees, and taxes may
cause deviation in performance between the spot price of a commodity and commodity futures. You should not assume that an ETF or
ETN that is linked to commodity futures will provide an effective hedge because of a negative correlation with equities or other asset

swaps, and other derivatives as well as non-investment-grade securities - give rise to substantial risk and are not available to nor suitable
for all investors. If you have any questions about whether you are eligible to enter into these transactions with Goldman Sachs, please
contact your sales representative. Foreign-currency-denominated securities are subject to fluctuations in exchange rates that could have
an adverse effect on the value or price of, or income derived from, the investment. In addition, investors in securities such as ADRs, the

values of which are influenced by foreign currencies, effectively assume currency risk.

## Document 435: The FOMC pause to reflect (2026-11-03)

Source PDF: all report pdf/The FOMC pause to reflect (2026-11-03).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-11-03
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#fomc #usd #us
The FOMC pause to reflect
By Elliot Clarke
Head of International Economics, Westpac
08:14 October 29 2026 Share
No change in October, though confidence in the economy's strength and two dissents point
to a hike in December.
The FOMC held at their October meeting, albeit with two dissents, Governor Cook and Cleveland Fed
President Hammack preferring to hike by 25bps at this meeting. The statement was short and, in the
press conference, Chair Warsh again declined to provide definitive guidance on the near-term policy

### Page 2

Related articles
outlook.
The Committee remains positive on economic activity, characterising it in the statement as “expanding
at a strong pace” (an upgrade from “solid” in September). Despite elevated uncertainty, “domestic
spending has been resilient”, underpinned by “strong” productivity growth and “robust” capital
investment. Chair Warsh subsequently also emphasised the breadth of economic growth outside of
tech-related investment. Labour demand and supply were represented as balanced and resilient in

both the statement and press conference.
The Committee refrained from spotlighting key price risks in the statement. However, inflation “remains
elevated” and the FOMC are determined to achieve a “timely” and sustainable return of annual
consumer inflation to 2.0%yr after 6 years above target.
Along with the two dissents, this guidance and the “accumulation of more information will help the
Committee determine the policy stance” suggests another 25bp hike is likely in December. Thereafter,

the FOMC will remain attentive to any broadening in inflation and related risks through 2027.
Towards the end of the press conference, Chair Warsh also announced a structural change in the
FOMC schedule. While there will still be 8 meetings in 2027, a meeting in the fall will focus on
monetary policy strategy versus the current stance of monetary policy. Chair Warsh then intends for 2
of the 8 meetings in 2028 to have this strategy focus. If realised, this will see the FOMC review and

vote on the current stance of monetary policy just 6 times per calendar year from 2028.

大館，此後因AI相關估值疑慮、科技股修正與長端利
率上行，全球科技股估值大幅修正，日經225指數在
10月23日盤中一度下探61,462點。
日本兩大指數在本月表現也呈現相對分化。日經225
權重權重集中於東京威力科創、愛德萬測試、軟銀集
團等半導體與 AI標的，在本波修正幅度較大；相反
的，TOPIX指數因金融、內需與防禦類股權重較高，
加上利率下行有利銀行以外的內需類股，回檔相對溫
和。也間接反映了10月的修正偏向是 「AI交易擁擠
的釋放」，而非全面性的景氣衰退定價。
債市方面，10年期日本公債殖利率在10月初一度升

至3.11%、創1999年以來新高 ，反映日本央行升
息、以及高市內閣財政擴張的雙重疑慮。
此後殖利率一路回落至 10月26日的2.82%、下行約
23個基點。這一下行幅度反映全球 AI相關信用風險
引發的避險需求。
重點摘要
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026/9，中國信託商業銀行整理
5
AI相關估值疑慮、科技股修正
• 10月日股受AI估值修正及長天
期利率波動影響而震盪下跌，
但修正主要集中於半導體與AI
權值股。
• TOPIX相對抗跌，顯示市場仍
未全面反映景氣衰退，而是資
金自擁擠交易部位撤出。

### Page 3

不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行並不負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資之風險，並就投
資結果自行負責。未經本公司許可，本資料及訊息不得逕行抄錄、翻印或另作派發。本行以上市場資訊與分析，不涉及特定投資標的之建議。
《日本出口維持韌性，內需動能出現疑慮》
經濟基本面部分，日本9月出口在半導體與AI相關需
求帶動下，連續13個月維持正成長 ，惟增速放緩至
16.7%、略低於預期；但進口年增卻升至18.9%，主
要因為進口能源價格大漲所致，導致貿易逆差擴大，
外需雖強卻未能轉化為淨出口貢獻。
內需與生產端則收高利高通膨壓力同步降溫。工業生
產增速自年中高點回落，製造業PMI降至七個月來最
低，整體擴張速度已低於上半年水準，細項中新訂單
與出口訂單成長同步放緩，能源與勞動成本則大幅走
升，對企業營運形成壓力。內需改善亦相當有限，零
售銷售下滑、失業率微升、求人求職比回落，反映勞
動市場略有降溫；所幸，實質薪資已連續八個月正成
長、消費者信心小幅回升。

整體而言，日本經濟仍處於「名目成長、實質停滯」
的格局，擴張主要仰賴出口，內需復甦力道偏弱，而
高油價帶來的逆差壓力與生產動能轉弱，將是後續成
長的主要考驗 。基於地緣與油價的不確定性 ，10月
日本央行暫停升息，維持利率不變。
重點摘要
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026/10，中國信託商業銀行整理
5
貨幣政策正常化以及財政疑慮推升日債殖利率走高
• 日本出口持續受惠半導體與AI
需求，惟能源價格上漲推升進
口成本與貿易逆差。工業生產、
PMI及勞動市場同步降溫，經
濟仍維持「出口強、內需弱」
格局，成長動能面臨考驗。

不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行並不負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資之風險，並就投
資結果自行負責。未經本公司許可，本資料及訊息不得逕行抄錄、翻印或另作派發。本行以上市場資訊與分析，不涉及特定投資標的之建議。
《日銀暫停升息，12月升息預期支撐利率高檔》
日本央行已於 9月會期將政策利率上調 25個基點至
1.25%，為1995年以來最高 ，也是三個月內第二
次、1990年以來最快的升息節奏。
隨著近期經濟數據 ，10月份日央如預期暫停升息一
碼，會議同步公布《經濟與物價形勢展望報告》，根
據央行經濟預測顯示潛在通膨「已大致達到2%」。
配合植田和男在10月16日記者會曾表示，若基礎通
膨進一步上升將是 「無法接受」的，並在10月19日
國會答詢時表示潛在通膨非常接近2%目標。
加以隨著國際油價再度上破110美元大關，短期政策
重點從「對抗通縮 」轉為「防止通膨超標 」，是以
12月再度升息一碼的機率已大幅提高。
另一方，高市內閣的食品消費稅減稅與國防支出增

加，使市場對財源安排存疑 。不過，10月19日高市
在國會承諾在下一財年預算中妥善管控國債發行規
模，使近期長端利率有所回落 。但12月下一財年預
算與國債發行計畫公布時，財政紀律的可信度將再受
檢驗，預期短線利率上行趨緩，維持高檔盤整。
重點摘要
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026/10，中國信託商業銀行整理
5
日本10年期利率回落至3.0%以內
• 日銀9月提前升息後，10月暫
停動作觀察經濟變化，但油價
上漲與通膨接近目標，提高年
底再升息機率。
• 另一方面，財政擴張與國債供
給疑慮仍在，預期長天期利率
維持高檔震盪。

不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行並不負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資之風險，並就投
資結果自行負責。未經本公司許可，本資料及訊息不得逕行抄錄、翻印或另作派發。本行以上市場資訊與分析，不涉及特定投資標的之建議。
《盤勢觀點-資金輪動加速，防禦成長族群接棒》
10月日本股市震盪 、類股表現分化 。市場資金明顯
由上半年漲幅龐大的AI硬體與半導體族群撤出，轉向
軟體服務、醫療保健及內需消費等防禦成長型產業。
其中，東京威力科創、SCREEN、瑞薩電子等指標股
普遍出現雙位數跌幅。相較之下，軟體服務類股大漲
17.9%，BayCurrent、SoftBank等個股表現突出。醫
藥生技、消費零售及娛樂觀光相關族群亦受惠於防禦
需求與內需消費韌性而表現相對亦強勢。值得注意的
是，銀行與非銀行金融股同步走弱，多家大型金融機
構跌幅介於8%至15%，顯示市場擔憂金融條件收緊
可能壓抑經濟活動與資本市場表現。
整體而言，10月日股市場風格由 「AI硬體主導」轉

向「防禦成長與內需消費主導」，同時對經濟基本面
疑慮小幅升溫 ，8~9月相對強勢的金融出現回調壓
力。展望未來，短期盤勢仍受利匯率壓力，以及科技
股評價調整影響 ，加以1H27預估日經225指數企業
獲利年增率轉為負成長，因此本行維持9月以來下調
日股至中性的看法不變，待國際油價回落以及年底年
度預算案底定後，可伺機布局。
重點摘要
5
• 市場風格由AI硬體轉向軟體服
務、醫療保健及內需消費等防
禦成長族群。半導體、金融與
工業設備股表現疲弱，反映投
資人開始關注高利率環境及企
業獲利成長放緩風險。
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026/10，中國信託商業銀行整理

不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行並不負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資之風險，並就投
資結果自行負責。未經本公司許可，本資料及訊息不得逕行抄錄、翻印或另作派發。本行以上市場資訊與分析，不涉及特定投資標的之建議。
《盤勢觀點-澳洲景氣放緩，股匯市轉向盤整》
隨著國際油價維持衝高，澳洲央行（RBA）於9月再
度升息將現金利率提高至 4.6%，官員雖表示若通膨
壓力持續，不排除進一步緊縮。但在高利率與高通膨
壓力持續積累下對景氣負面壓力逐漸加大。
資料顯示，澳洲9月失業率升至4.72%，已連續兩個
月 落 在 澳 央 主 席 認 為 可 有 效 壓 抑 通 膨 的 失 業 率
4.5~5.0%區間；而通膨方面 ，9月份澳洲 CPI雖由
4.0%升至4.3%，但核心CPI僅有2.2%，加以住房通
膨明顯回落，因此本行預期澳央轉向觀望、本輪升息
暫時告終，2027年政策轉向觀望。
外需方面，中國經濟基本面持續低迷，消費與投資不
振亦難以挹注澳洲出口動能，外需對澳幣支撐力量有

限，加以預期聯準會在 12月再度升息 ，將呈現「利
差有撐但優勢遞減」格局，因此預估澳幣短線維持在
0.7~0.71區間盤整。
綜上，持續升息後澳洲基本面轉弱，但高油價壓力又
迫使央行貨幣政策難以轉向寬鬆，加以企業獲利未見
改善動能，澳洲股市難以樂觀，本行維持中性展望。
重點摘要
• 高油價迫使澳洲央行維持偏
鷹態度 ，但高利率已逐步壓
抑就業與內需。
• 中國需求偏弱限制出口前景 ，
加上美澳利差優勢收斂 ，預
期澳幣短期維持區間震盪 ，
澳股展望偏中性。

今月の景気判断 ～持ち直している
【今月のポイント】
 東海経済は、持ち直している
 7月の生産（東海3県、季節調整値）は前月
比＋2.2％と2ヵ月ぶりに増加した。生産水
準も高く、増加基調にあるとみられる
 輸出は総じてみると足踏み状態。先行きは
持ち直しを見込むが、米・イランの対立長
期化などで足踏みとなるリスクがある
 日銀短観9月調査の業況判断DI（「良い」
－「悪い」）は、製造業が改善する一方、非
製造業は悪化した。設備投資も前年度比
増加が見込まれている
 8月の小売販売額は名目で2ヵ月連続で
前年比プラスとなったが、実質では3ヵ月
連続でマイナス。一方、新車販売台数は
5ヵ月連続で前年比プラスとなった
現状

生産 増加している

雇用 持ち直しの動きがみられる
賃金 持ち直している
